Generate a coding prompt for this task: AVCaptureSession with AVCaptureVideoPreviewLayer via FlutterPlatformView — Phase: Native Camera & Sensor Pipeline, Track: iOS Native (Swift)

------------------------------------------------------------------------------->>>

# NEW: iOS Camera Preview — AVCaptureSession + AVCaptureVideoPreviewLayer via FlutterPlatformView
# Product: MayasabhaXR — maya-viewer (Flutter client, iOS native)
# Scope: New Feature / Native iOS (Swift) + Flutter embedding
# Phase: Native Camera & Sensor Pipeline
# Track: iOS Native (Swift)
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Bridge a live iOS back-camera preview into Flutter: an `AVCaptureSession` feeding an `AVCaptureVideoPreviewLayer` hosted in a `UIView`, embedded via a `FlutterPlatformView` (the iOS `UiKitView`). This is the iOS counterpart to the Android CameraX preview task — same purpose, same Flutter-facing start/stop/lifecycle contract where possible, different stack. It is the PREVIEW only; photo/burst capture, focus/exposure lock, and sensors are separate iOS tasks in this phase.

iOS-specific weight (vs Android): session configuration/start/stop is BLOCKING and must run on a dedicated session queue (never main); iOS capture sessions get INTERRUPTED (calls, other apps, multitasking) and must be handled; preview rotation is set on the connection (with the iOS 17 `videoOrientation` → `videoRotationAngle` change).

- [ ] Configure an `AVCaptureSession` (back camera input, sensible preset) on a dedicated serial session queue
- [ ] Host an `AVCaptureVideoPreviewLayer` in a `UIView`, embedded via `FlutterPlatformViewFactory`/`FlutterPlatformView` (`UiKitView`)
- [ ] start/stop the session on the session queue; provide start/stop/dispose to Flutter
- [ ] Handle session interruptions and runtime errors (resume when possible)
- [ ] Handle rotation (preview connection orientation; iOS 17 API nuance) and correct layer↔view sizing
- [ ] Assume camera permission is granted (P2 gate + `NSCameraUsageDescription`); handle not-granted gracefully (no request here)
- [ ] Stop/release on background/dispose; restart on resume; never leak the camera

## Files to Inspect First

> Adjust these paths to the real repo layout. They reflect the assumed conventions in the Assumptions section — confirm before the agent edits anything.

1. The Android CameraX preview task — match the Flutter-facing contract (start/stop/dispose, lifecycle semantics) so the Dart capture screen treats both platforms consistently (Android: `AndroidView`/`Texture`; iOS: `UiKitView`)
2. The P2 iOS permission tasks — `NSCameraUsageDescription` in Info.plist (required or the session/camera access misbehaves) and the permission gate ensuring camera is granted before this screen
3. `ios/Runner/AppDelegate.swift` / the plugin registrant — registering the `FlutterPlatformViewFactory`
4. The capture screen (Flutter) — how it embeds `UiKitView` and calls start/stop
5. iOS deployment target — `videoRotationAngle` (iOS 17+) vs deprecated `videoOrientation`; branch accordingly
6. Existing `FlutterMethodChannel`/platform-view conventions

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Session + input on a session queue
- Create a dedicated serial `DispatchQueue` (the standard `sessionQueue`). Do ALL `AVCaptureSession` configuration and `startRunning()`/`stopRunning()` on it — these are blocking and must NOT run on the main thread.
- Configure: `beginConfiguration()`, set a `sessionPreset` (resolution/quality appropriate for preview), add the back camera input (`AVCaptureDevice.default(.builtInWideAngleCamera, for: .video, position: .back)` → `AVCaptureDeviceInput`), `commitConfiguration()`.

### Step 2: Preview layer in a UIView
- Create an `AVCaptureVideoPreviewLayer(session:)`, set `videoGravity` (`.resizeAspectFill` to fill or `.resizeAspect` to fit — confirm, no stretch), and add it as a sublayer of a hosting `UIView`.
- In the view's `layoutSubviews`, set the preview layer's `frame` to the view's `bounds` so it resizes correctly within the Flutter-embedded view.

### Step 3: FlutterPlatformView embedding
- Implement a `FlutterPlatformViewFactory` whose `create(...)` returns a `FlutterPlatformView` whose `view()` is the hosting `UIView`. Register the factory with the plugin registrar under a view type id. The Flutter side embeds it via `UiKitView(viewType: ...)`.

### Step 4: start/stop + lifecycle
- Expose start/stop/dispose over a `FlutterMethodChannel` (parity with the Android contract). `start` → `sessionQueue.async { session.startRunning() }`; `stop` → `stopRunning()` on the session queue.
- On Flutter dispose / app background: stop the session and release input/session; on foreground/resume: restart (re-`startRunning` on the session queue). Stop the camera when not visible (power + release for other users). Clean up observers on dispose.

### Step 5: Interruptions + runtime errors (iOS-specific)
- Observe `AVCaptureSession.wasInterruptedNotification` (read `AVCaptureSessionInterruptionReasonKey` — e.g. video device in use by another client, not available in multitasking) and `interruptionEndedNotification` — resume the session when the interruption ends.
- Observe `runtimeErrorNotification` (`AVCaptureSessionRuntimeError`) — attempt restart for recoverable errors (`error.isRecoverable`-style handling), report otherwise. Surface a state to Flutter (e.g. interrupted/running/error) so the UI can show "camera unavailable / resuming".

### Step 6: Rotation + orientation
- Update the preview connection orientation on device rotation so the feed stays upright. iOS 17+: use `AVCaptureConnection.videoRotationAngle` (check `isVideoRotationAngleSupported`); pre-iOS 17: set `videoOrientation` from the interface/device orientation. Branch on availability. Keep the layer sizing in sync with the view.

### Step 7: Permission guard (no request here)
- Assume camera permission is granted (P2 gate). Defensively: if `AVCaptureDevice.authorizationStatus(for: .video)` is not `.authorized` at setup, fail gracefully (surface an error state to Flutter) — do NOT call `requestAccess` here (P2 owns the request; `NSCameraUsageDescription` is a P2 task). If no back camera/device is available, error gracefully.

## API / Embedding Contract

```
PlatformView (UiKitView): viewType "mayasabhaxr/camera_preview_ios"
  hosts a UIView with an AVCaptureVideoPreviewLayer

MethodChannel: "mayasabhaxr/camera_preview" (parity with Android)
  start()   -> session.startRunning() on sessionQueue
  stop()    -> stopRunning() on sessionQueue (restartable)
  dispose() -> stop + release session/input, remove observers

State events (interrupted | running | error | unavailable) surfaced to Flutter.

Guarantees: session config/start/stop on the session queue (never main); preview layer on main, sized to bounds;
interruptions/runtime-errors handled; rotation correct; camera released on background/dispose; no permission request here.
```

## What NOT to Do

- Do NOT call `startRunning()`/`stopRunning()` or session configuration on the main thread — use a dedicated session queue (blocking calls hang/jank the UI).
- Do NOT manipulate the preview layer / UI off the main thread (layer attach/size on main; session ops on the session queue).
- Do NOT request camera permission here or assume `NSCameraUsageDescription` — P2 owns the request and the usage string; assume granted, handle denied gracefully.
- Do NOT ignore session interruptions/runtime errors — observe and handle (resume when possible); iOS sessions WILL be interrupted.
- Do NOT leak the camera/session — stop on background/dispose, release input/session, remove notification observers.
- Do NOT skip rotation handling — set the connection orientation (iOS 17 `videoRotationAngle` vs older `videoOrientation`).
- Do NOT stretch the preview — set `videoGravity` and keep the layer frame = view bounds.
- Do NOT implement photo/video capture, focus/exposure lock, or sensors here — separate iOS tasks.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Camera permission not granted at setup (defensive) → graceful error state, no crash, no request here.
- [ ] No back camera / device unavailable → graceful error, no crash.
- [ ] Session interrupted (incoming call, other app using camera, iPad multitasking) → handled; resumes on interruption-ended.
- [ ] `AVCaptureSessionRuntimeError` → recoverable errors restart the session; others reported.
- [ ] Device rotation → preview stays upright via connection orientation (correct API per iOS version).
- [ ] App background→foreground → session stopped then restarted cleanly; camera released while backgrounded.
- [ ] Flutter view resized → preview layer frame tracks the view bounds (layoutSubviews); no stretch/misalignment.
- [ ] Rapid create/dispose → session/observers cleaned up; no leak; no callback after dispose.
- [ ] iOS version below the `videoRotationAngle` threshold → falls back to `videoOrientation`.

## Constraints

- Session configuration/start/stop MUST run on a dedicated session queue, never the main thread; preview layer/UI on main.
- The preview MUST be embedded via `FlutterPlatformView` (`UiKitView`) hosting an `AVCaptureVideoPreviewLayer`.
- Session interruptions and runtime errors MUST be observed and handled (resume when possible), with state surfaced to Flutter.
- Rotation MUST keep the preview upright via the connection orientation (correct API for the iOS version); the layer frame MUST track the view bounds.
- The camera MUST be released on background/dispose and restarted on resume; observers removed; no leak.
- Camera permission MUST NOT be requested here (P2 owns it); not-granted/unavailable fails gracefully.
- The Flutter-facing contract SHOULD parallel the Android preview task (start/stop/dispose) for a consistent capture screen.
- MUST stay scoped to preview — no capture, focus/exposure lock, or sensor work.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] The capture screen shows a live, correctly-oriented, unstretched back-camera preview on iOS via the embedded platform view.
- [ ] Session config/start/stop run on the session queue (no main-thread blocking/jank on start); the preview layer is managed on main.
- [ ] An incoming call / another camera client / multitasking interrupts the session and the preview resumes when the interruption ends.
- [ ] A recoverable runtime error restarts the session; non-recoverable errors surface a clear state; the UI can reflect interrupted/error.
- [ ] Rotating the device keeps the preview upright (correct API per iOS version); resizing the Flutter view resizes the preview without stretch.
- [ ] Backgrounding releases the camera; foregrounding restarts the preview; dispose releases session/input and removes observers (no leak).
- [ ] Permission is not requested here; denied/unavailable produces a graceful error state, not a crash.
- [ ] The Flutter start/stop/dispose contract parallels the Android preview task.
- [ ] No capture/lock/sensor logic is implemented here.
- [ ] Builds clean; the Flutter wrapper passes `flutter analyze`.

## Testing Instructions

1. On a device (camera granted), open the capture screen → confirm a live, upright, unstretched back-camera preview.
2. Trigger an interruption (incoming call, or open another camera app / iPad split view) → confirm the preview pauses and resumes when the interruption ends (interruption notifications handled).
3. Force a runtime error scenario → confirm recoverable restart / clear error state surfaced to Flutter.
4. Rotate the device → confirm the preview stays upright (verify on iOS 17+ `videoRotationAngle` and an older OS `videoOrientation` if supported).

8. Launch with permission denied (defensive) → confirm graceful error, no crash, no permission prompt from here.
9. Profile start → confirm no main-thread block/jank (session queue used).
10. Build + `flutter analyze` → confirm clean.

## Assumptions

- **iOS counterpart to Android preview:** Assumes parity with the CameraX preview task's Flutter contract (start/stop/dispose, lifecycle) so the Dart capture screen is consistent; iOS embeds `UiKitView`, Android `AndroidView`/`Texture`.
- **Permission precondition:** Assumes P2 ensures camera is granted and `NSCameraUsageDescription` is present (the iOS Info.plist task). This task consumes that; it never requests. Defensive handling for not-granted/unavailable.
- **Embedding approach:** `FlutterPlatformView` (`UiKitView`) hosting `AVCaptureVideoPreviewLayer` is idiomatic and performant on iOS (unlike Android, where texture was often preferred). No texture/platform-view fork here — the task specifies the platform view.
- **iOS version:** Assumes handling both `videoRotationAngle` (iOS 17+) and the deprecated `videoOrientation` (older) per the deployment target. Confirm the minimum iOS version.
- **Preset/fill:** Assumes a preview-appropriate `sessionPreset` and a confirmed `videoGravity` (fill vs fit, no stretch). Capture resolution is a separate iOS capture task.
- **Scope:** Preview only. iOS photo/burst capture, focus/exposure lock, and motion/sensors are separate iOS tasks; this neither captures nor controls exposure nor reads sensors.
- **Stack:** Swift + AVFoundation + `FlutterPlatformView`/`FlutterMethodChannel`. Adjust to the app's structure.

Generate a coding prompt for this task: Manual focus/exposure lock via AVCaptureDevice.FocusMode/ExposureMode — Phase: Native Camera & Sensor Pipeline, Track: iOS Native (Swift)

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------->>>



# NEW: iOS Manual Focus / Exposure Lock via AVCaptureDevice (FocusMode/ExposureMode)
# Product: MayasabhaXR — maya-viewer (Flutter client, iOS native)
# Scope: New Feature / Native iOS (Swift) — AVCaptureDevice control
# Phase: Native Camera & Sensor Pipeline
# Track: iOS Native (Swift)
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Add manual focus and exposure LOCK to the existing iOS capture session (from the iOS preview task) via `AVCaptureDevice` focus/exposure modes, exposed to Flutter. Locking AF/AE keeps focus and exposure stable during a capture session — needed for consistent frames in 3D/photogrammetry. This is the iOS counterpart to the Android Camera2 focus/exposure-lock task; keep the Flutter-facing contract parallel so the capture screen drives both platforms the same way.

This operates on the SAME `AVCaptureDevice` bound in the preview task's session — it does NOT create or reconfigure the session. Core = exposure lock + focus hold; manual focus (`lensPosition`), custom exposure (`duration`/`iso`), and white-balance lock are confirmable, capability-gated extensions.

The dominant iOS rule: **every device-property mutation MUST be wrapped in `lockForConfiguration()` / `unlockForConfiguration()`** — mutating `focusMode`/`exposureMode`/etc. without it throws. Run configuration on the session queue (consistent with the preview task).

- [ ] Lock/unlock exposure via `exposureMode = .locked` / `.continuousAutoExposure`
- [ ] Hold/lock focus via `focusMode = .locked`; optional manual `setFocusModeLocked(lensPosition:)` if supported
- [ ] Optional custom exposure `setExposureModeCustom(duration:iso:)` and white-balance lock, capability-gated
- [ ] Capability-detect each control; expose only supported ones; degrade gracefully
- [ ] Wrap ALL mutations in `lockForConfiguration()`/`unlockForConfiguration()` (handle the throw); run on the session queue
- [ ] Expose lock/unlock + capability query to Flutter (parallel to Android)
- [ ] Define lock behavior across session interruption/restart (parallel to Android)

## Files to Inspect First

> Adjust these paths to the real repo layout. They reflect the assumed conventions in the Assumptions section — confirm before the agent edits anything.

1. The iOS preview task — owns the `AVCaptureSession`, the `AVCaptureDevice`, and the session queue; this task mutates that device on that queue
2. The Android focus/exposure-lock task — match the Flutter-facing contract (`getCameraControlCapabilities`, `setExposureLock`, `setFocusLocked`/`setManualFocusDistance`, `unlockAll`) so the Dart layer is platform-consistent
3. The preview task's interruption/restart handling — lock state must coordinate with session restart (resets vs re-applies)
4. The capture screen (Flutter) — how it calls lock/unlock and reads capabilities
5. iOS deployment target — availability of `setExposureModeCustom`, custom lens position APIs

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Get the device + capabilities
- Obtain the active `AVCaptureDevice` from the preview task's session input. Capability-detect (expose only what's supported):
  - Exposure lock: `isExposureModeSupported(.locked)`.
  - Custom exposure: `isExposureModeSupported(.custom)` (+ `activeFormat` `minExposureDuration`/`maxExposureDuration`, `activeFormat.minISO`/`maxISO`).
  - Focus hold: `isFocusModeSupported(.locked)`.
  - Manual lens position: `isLockingFocusWithCustomLensPositionSupported`.
  - White-balance lock: `isWhiteBalanceModeSupported(.locked)`.
- Provide `getCameraControlCapabilities()` so Flutter shows/hides controls.

### Step 2: The lockForConfiguration pattern (critical)
- For EVERY mutation:
  ```swift
  sessionQueue.async {
    do {
      try device.lockForConfiguration()
      // mutate focusMode/exposureMode/lensPosition/etc.
      device.unlockForConfiguration()
    } catch { /* report; do not crash */ }
  }
  ```
- Never mutate device properties without `lockForConfiguration()`. Always `unlockForConfiguration()` (even on early return). Handle the thrown error.

### Step 3: Exposure lock
- Lock: `device.exposureMode = .locked`. Unlock: `.continuousAutoExposure` (restore auto).
- Optional custom: `device.setExposureModeCustom(duration:iso:completionHandler:)` with values clamped to the `activeFormat` ranges (only if `.custom` supported).

### Step 4: Focus hold / manual focus
- Hold: `device.focusMode = .locked` (freezes the current lens position). Unlock: `.continuousAutoFocus`.
- Manual lens position (if `isLockingFocusWithCustomLensPositionSupported`): `device.setFocusModeLocked(lensPosition:completionHandler:)` where `lensPosition ∈ [0,1]` (NORMALIZED — 0 ≈ near, 1 ≈ far; NOT diopters like Android). Clamp inputs to `[0,1]`.

### Step 5: White-balance lock (optional)
- `device.whiteBalanceMode = .locked` if supported; unlock to `.continuousAutoWhiteBalance`.

### Step 6: Expose to Flutter (parallel to Android)
`FlutterMethodChannel` (parity with the Android lock task):
- `getCameraControlCapabilities()` → `{ aeLock, manualFocus, customExposure, awbLock, lensPositionRange:{0,1}?, exposureDurationRange?, isoRange? }`
- `setExposureLock(bool)` / `setAutoWhiteBalanceLock(bool)`
- `setFocusLocked(bool)` and/or `setManualFocusDistance(double)` → mapped to `lensPosition` (note the normalized [0,1] semantics; document the unit difference from Android)
- `unlockAll()` → restore continuous auto AE/AF (/AWB)

### Step 7: Session restart behavior (define + implement)
- On session interruption/restart (preview task), DECIDE and document (parallel to Android): default RESET to auto on restart (a restarted session reverts to continuous auto), capture flow re-locks as needed; alternative = re-apply stored lock state on restart. Confirm with the capture design.
- Lock calls while no device/session is active → defined error/no-op, no crash.

## API / MethodChannel Contract

```
Channel: "mayasabhaxr/camera_preview" (or the app's camera channel; parallel to Android)
  getCameraControlCapabilities() -> { aeLock, manualFocus, customExposure, awbLock,
                                       lensPositionRange?, exposureDurationRange?, isoRange? }
  setExposureLock(locked)         -> exposureMode .locked / .continuousAutoExposure
  setAutoWhiteBalanceLock(locked) -> whiteBalanceMode .locked (if supported)
  setFocusLocked(locked)          -> focusMode .locked / .continuousAutoFocus
  setManualFocusDistance(pos)     -> setFocusModeLocked(lensPosition: clamp01(pos))  [if supported]
  unlockAll()                     -> restore continuous auto AE/AF/AWB

Guarantees: mutations wrapped in lockForConfiguration/unlock on the session queue; capability-gated;
operates on the existing device; lensPosition normalized [0,1]; restart behavior documented.
```

## What NOT to Do

- Do NOT mutate `AVCaptureDevice` properties without `lockForConfiguration()`/`unlockForConfiguration()` — it throws/crashes. Always lock, always unlock, handle the error.
- Do NOT expose controls the device doesn't support — capability-gate (`isExposureModeSupported`, `isLockingFocusWithCustomLensPositionSupported`, etc.).
- Do NOT use diopters for manual focus — iOS `lensPosition` is normalized `[0,1]`; clamp and document the difference from Android.
- Do NOT recreate/reconfigure the session to apply controls — mutate the existing device from the preview task.
- Do NOT run configuration off the session queue or block the main thread.
- Do NOT leave the device locked unintentionally — `unlockAll` restores continuous auto; define restart behavior.
- Do NOT request camera permission or manage session lifecycle here — preview task / P2.
- Do NOT implement capture or sensors here — separate iOS tasks.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Device without manual lens position → `manualFocus:false`; offer `.locked` hold only; `setManualFocusDistance` no-ops gracefully.
- [ ] Device without `.custom` exposure → `customExposure:false`; AE `.locked` only.
- [ ] `lockForConfiguration()` throws → reported; no crash; no partial mutation left without unlock.
- [ ] Session interrupted/restarted → lock state per the documented decision (default reset to auto).
- [ ] `lensPosition`/`duration`/`iso` out of range → clamped to supported ranges.
- [ ] Lock requested with no active device/session → defined error/no-op.
- [ ] Rapid lock/unlock toggling → last write wins; consistent final state; no crash.
- [ ] White-balance unsupported → `awbLock:false`; no-op gracefully.

## Constraints

- Every device mutation MUST be wrapped in `lockForConfiguration()`/`unlockForConfiguration()` (with error handling), on the session queue; never the main thread.
- MUST operate on the existing session's `AVCaptureDevice`; no session recreate/reconfigure.
- MUST capability-gate every control; unsupported controls not offered and safe if called.
- Manual focus MUST use normalized `lensPosition [0,1]` (clamped); custom exposure clamped to `activeFormat` ranges; `unlockAll` restores continuous auto.
- Session-restart lock behavior MUST be defined/documented (default reset to auto).
- The Flutter contract SHOULD parallel the Android lock task (with the lensPosition unit difference documented).
- MUST stay scoped to focus/exposure (+optional WB) control — no permission, session lifecycle, capture, or sensors.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] `getCameraControlCapabilities()` accurately reports AE lock, focus hold, manual lens position, custom exposure, WB lock, and ranges.
- [ ] Locking exposure freezes the preview's exposure under changing light; unlocking resumes continuous auto.
- [ ] Locking focus holds the current focus (no hunting); unlocking resumes continuous AF.
- [ ] On a capable device, `setManualFocusDistance` maps to a normalized `lensPosition` and changes focus predictably; out-of-range clamped.
- [ ] On a device lacking a control, it's reported unsupported and not offered; defensive calls no-op without crashing.
- [ ] Every mutation is wrapped in lock/unlock on the session queue; a `lockForConfiguration` failure is reported, not crashed.
- [ ] `unlockAll()` restores continuous auto AE/AF (/AWB).
- [ ] Session interrupt/restart behaves per the documented decision (default reset to auto).
- [ ] The Flutter contract parallels Android; the lensPosition-vs-diopters difference is documented.
- [ ] No capture/sensor logic here.
- [ ] Builds clean; the Flutter wrapper passes `flutter analyze`.

## Testing Instructions

1. With the iOS preview running, lock exposure → move between bright/dark areas → confirm exposure stays fixed; unlock → confirm it resumes auto.
2. Lock focus on a subject → move the camera → confirm no focus hunting; unlock → confirm continuous AF resumes.
3. On a capable device: sweep `setManualFocusDistance` 0→1 → confirm focus changes near→far; pass out-of-range → confirm clamping.
4. On a device lacking custom lens position / custom exposure → confirm those report unsupported and no-op gracefully.
5. Force `lockForConfiguration()` to fail (e.g. during an interruption) → confirm a reported error, no crash, no dangling unlocked state.
6. Interrupt/restart the session → confirm lock state matches the documented behavior (default reset to auto).
7. Rapidly toggle lock/unlock → confirm a consistent final state, no crash.
8. Confirm all mutations run on the session queue (no main-thread config); profile for jank.
9. Build + `flutter analyze` → confirm clean.

## Assumptions

- **Builds on iOS preview:** Assumes the preview task owns the session/device/session-queue; this task mutates that device on that queue and adds control only. Blocked on the iOS preview if absent.
- **iOS counterpart parity:** Assumes the Flutter contract parallels the Android lock task; the key semantic difference is manual focus = normalized `lensPosition [0,1]` (iOS) vs diopters (Android) — documented for the Dart layer.
- **Scope:** Core = AE lock + focus hold. Manual lens position, custom exposure, and WB lock are confirmable, capability-gated extensions. Confirm which the capture UX needs.
- **Restart behavior:** Defaulted to reset-to-auto on session restart; if the capture flow needs lock to persist across interruption, re-apply stored state instead. Confirm.
- **Capture consistency rationale:** Assumes locking is for stable capture frames (photogrammetry), as on Android. If it's just tap-to-focus UX, the surface simplifies.
- **Stack:** Swift + AVFoundation `AVCaptureDevice` + `FlutterMethodChannel`. Adjust to the app's structure.

Generate a coding prompt for this task: CMDeviceMotion.attitude (quaternion → yaw/pitch/roll) via CMMotionManager — Phase: Native Camera & Sensor Pipeline, Track: iOS Native (Swift)

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------->>>>>>>

# NATIVE (iOS/Swift): CMDeviceMotion.attitude (Quaternion → Yaw/Pitch/Roll) via CMMotionManager
# Product: maya-viewer (iOS Native — Sensor Pipeline)
# Scope: New Feature (Native Module)
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Implement the iOS-native device-motion source that reads `CMDeviceMotion.attitude` from `CMMotionManager`, converts the attitude **quaternion → yaw/pitch/roll** (radians and degrees), and streams it to Dart over the existing `sensorStream` `FlutterEventChannel`. This is the iOS half of the `sensorStream` contract defined in the Native Module Stubs foundation; the Flutter wrapper (`SensorStreamChannel`) already consumes this channel. Updates must start on subscribe, stop on cancel, throttle to a configured rate, and degrade cleanly on devices/states without device motion.

- [ ] Register a native `FlutterEventChannel` named exactly `sensorStream`
- [ ] On listen, start `CMMotionManager` device-motion updates with a fixed reference frame and update interval
- [ ] Convert each frame's attitude quaternion to yaw/pitch/roll (handle gimbal-lock edge with `atan2`-based formulas)
- [ ] Emit a payload matching the agreed `SensorStreamPayload` shape the Dart side decodes
- [ ] On cancel/dispose, stop updates and release the motion queue
- [ ] Report `deviceMotionSupported` and emit nothing (or a single unsupported marker) when device motion is unavailable

## Files to Inspect First

1. The iOS Runner / plugin registration (`ios/Runner/AppDelegate.swift` or the plugin's `register(with:)`) — where Flutter channels are wired; follow the existing channel-registration pattern
2. The Flutter side `lib/native/sensor_stream.dart` — confirm the exact payload keys the Dart `SensorStreamPayload.fromMap` expects (`timestamp`, `orientation.alpha/beta/gamma`, `accelerometer.x/y/z`, `deviceMotionSupported`)
3. The foundation EventChannel contract (`SENSOR_STREAM_EVENT = "sensorStream"`) — the channel name must match byte-for-byte
4. `ios/Podfile` / capabilities — confirm CoreMotion is linkable (it ships with iOS; no extra pod) and no motion-usage description is missing from `Info.plist`

## Payload Contract (must match the Dart decoder)

Emit a `[String: Any]` dictionary per frame:



{

"timestamp": <Int ms epoch>,

"orientation": {           // mapped from yaw/pitch/roll — see reconciliation note

"alpha": <Double degrees>,   // yaw   (Z)

"beta":  <Double degrees>,   // pitch (X)

"gamma": <Double degrees>    // roll  (Y)

},

"accelerometer": {          // user acceleration (g), from deviceMotion.userAcceleration

"x": <Double>, "y": <Double>, "z": <Double>

},

"deviceMotionSupported": <Bool>

}




Reconciliation: the contract payload uses W3C `alpha/beta/gamma` keys. Map yaw→alpha, pitch→beta, roll→gamma (degrees) so the existing Dart model decodes unchanged. Also include raw yaw/pitch/roll under an optional `attitude` sub-key if the capture pipeline needs radians (see assumptions) — but do not break the existing keys.

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Stream handler class
In `ios/Runner/Sensors/SensorStreamHandler.swift`, implement `FlutterStreamHandler`:

```swift
import CoreMotion
import Flutter

final class SensorStreamHandler: NSObject, FlutterStreamHandler {
    private let motionManager = CMMotionManager()
    private let queue = OperationQueue()
    private var eventSink: FlutterEventSink?

    func onListen(withArguments arguments: Any?, eventSink events: @escaping FlutterEventSink) -> FlutterError? {
        self.eventSink = events
        startUpdates()
        return nil
    }

    func onCancel(withArguments arguments: Any?) -> FlutterError? {
        stopUpdates()
        self.eventSink = nil
        return nil
    }
    // start/stop + conversion below
}
```

### Step 2: Start updates
```swift
private func startUpdates() {
    guard motionManager.isDeviceMotionAvailable else {
        // emit a single unsupported marker so Dart knows device motion is off
        eventSink?(["timestamp": Int(Date().timeIntervalSince1970 * 1000),
                    "orientation": ["alpha": 0, "beta": 0, "gamma": 0],
                    "accelerometer": ["x": 0, "y": 0, "z": 0],
                    "deviceMotionSupported": false])
        return
    }
    motionManager.deviceMotionUpdateInterval = 1.0 / Double(updateHz) // e.g. 30 Hz
    queue.maxConcurrentOperationCount = 1
    motionManager.startDeviceMotionUpdates(
        using: .xArbitraryZVertical, // fixed reference frame; consistent yaw baseline
        to: queue
    ) { [weak self] motion, error in
        guard let self = self, let motion = motion, error == nil else { return }
        let payload = self.makePayload(from: motion)
        // hop back to main thread for the sink (Flutter sinks expect main-isolate delivery)
        DispatchQueue.main.async { self.eventSink?(payload) }
    }
}

private func stopUpdates() {
    if motionManager.isDeviceMotionActive {
        motionManager.stopDeviceMotionUpdates()
    }
    queue.cancelAllOperations()
}
```

### Step 3: Quaternion → yaw/pitch/roll
```swift
private func eulerFromQuaternion(_ q: CMQuaternion) -> (yaw: Double, pitch: Double, roll: Double) {
    // ZYX intrinsic convention; use atan2/asin with clamping to avoid NaN at poles.
    let w = q.w, x = q.x, y = q.y, z = q.z

    // roll (X)
    let sinrCosp = 2 * (w * x + y * z)
    let cosrCosp = 1 - 2 * (x * x + y * y)
    let roll = atan2(sinrCosp, cosrCosp)

    // pitch (Y)
    let sinp = 2 * (w * y - z * x)
    let pitch = abs(sinp) >= 1 ? copysign(Double.pi / 2, sinp) : asin(sinp) // clamp at gimbal lock

    // yaw (Z)
    let sinyCosp = 2 * (w * z + x * y)
    let cosyCosp = 1 - 2 * (y * y + z * z)
    let yaw = atan2(sinyCosp, cosyCosp)

    return (yaw, pitch, roll)
}
```
(Confirm the axis mapping against the capture pipeline's expectation — note in assumptions. The key requirement is a stable, NaN-free conversion using `atan2`/clamped `asin`.)

### Step 4: Build payload
```swift
private func makePayload(from motion: CMDeviceMotion) -> [String: Any] {
    let (yaw, pitch, roll) = eulerFromQuaternion(motion.attitude.quaternion)
    let rad2deg = 180.0 / Double.pi
    let acc = motion.userAcceleration
    return [
        "timestamp": Int(Date().timeIntervalSince1970 * 1000),
        "orientation": [
            "alpha": yaw * rad2deg,
            "beta":  pitch * rad2deg,
            "gamma": roll * rad2deg
        ],
        "accelerometer": ["x": acc.x, "y": acc.y, "z": acc.z],
        "deviceMotionSupported": true,
        "attitude": ["yaw": yaw, "pitch": pitch, "roll": roll] // radians, optional extra
    ]
}
```

### Step 5: Register the channel
In the app delegate / plugin registrar:
```swift
let messenger = controller.binaryMessenger
let sensorChannel = FlutterEventChannel(name: "sensorStream", binaryMessenger: messenger)
sensorChannel.setStreamHandler(SensorStreamHandler())
```
Hold a strong reference to the handler so it isn't deallocated.

### Step 6: Lifecycle hardening
- Stop updates on `onCancel` and ensure `stopUpdates()` is idempotent
- Optionally observe app background/foreground to pause/resume updates (device motion in background is restricted; stopping on background avoids wasted battery) — keep simple; document choice

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT rename or alter the `sensorStream` channel name — it must match the foundation contract and the Dart wrapper exactly
- Do NOT change the existing payload keys (`timestamp`, `orientation.alpha/beta/gamma`, `accelerometer`, `deviceMotionSupported`) — only additive keys (`attitude`) are allowed
- Do NOT call the `FlutterEventSink` off the main thread — always dispatch sink calls to main
- Do NOT create multiple `CMMotionManager` instances — one per handler is the documented best practice
- Do NOT implement the capture/photo logic here — this task is device motion only (`captureEvents` is separate)
- Do NOT add a CoreMotion CocoaPod — it is a system framework

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] `isDeviceMotionAvailable == false` (simulator, restricted device) → emit a single `deviceMotionSupported: false` marker, do not crash, do not spin a dead loop
- [ ] Motion permission denied (`Permission.sensors` from the Flutter permissions gate) → updates may yield no data; ensure no crash and that Dart still sees `deviceMotionSupported` correctly (note: CoreMotion attitude generally doesn't require runtime permission, but pedometer/activity does — confirm)
- [ ] Gimbal lock at pitch ±90° → `asin` clamped via `copysign`, no NaN emitted
- [ ] Rapid listen → cancel → listen cycles → no leaked update callbacks; `stopUpdates` is idempotent
- [ ] App backgrounded mid-stream → updates paused/stopped, resumed on foreground (or cleanly stopped)
- [ ] Handler deallocated while updates running → `[weak self]` guard prevents use-after-free; updates stopped in deinit
- [ ] Very high update rate requested → clamp `updateHz` to a sane max (e.g. ≤ 60) to protect battery/CPU on low-end devices
- [ ] Sink called after cancel (in-flight callback) → guard with `eventSink != nil` / weak self so no emission post-cancel

## Constraints

- Exactly one `CMMotionManager` instance per stream handler; use a dedicated `OperationQueue`, not the main queue, for updates — but deliver sink events on the main thread
- Quaternion→Euler conversion must be NaN-free (use `atan2` and clamped `asin`)
- Use a fixed attitude reference frame (`.xArbitraryZVertical`) for a consistent yaw baseline across sessions
- Update interval/rate comes from a constant (default 30 Hz), clamped to a max; not hardcoded inline in multiple places
- Emitted payload keys must remain backward-compatible with the existing Dart `SensorStreamPayload.fromMap`
- No retained strong reference cycles (handler ↔ closures): use `[weak self]`

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] The `sensorStream` `FlutterEventChannel` is registered and the Dart `SensorStreamChannel().stream` receives decoded `SensorStreamPayload` values on a physical device
- [ ] Yaw/pitch/roll are correct: rotating the device about each axis changes the expected `alpha/beta/gamma` value monotonically and stays NaN-free, including near ±90° pitch
- [ ] Updates start on subscribe and stop on cancel; no events emitted after cancel
- [ ] On the simulator / unsupported device, a single `deviceMotionSupported: false` payload is emitted with no crash
- [ ] No main-thread blocking; sink events are delivered on main while sampling runs on the operation queue
- [ ] Listen→cancel→listen cycles leak no callbacks (verified with Instruments / repeated toggling)
- [ ] Update rate is throttled to the configured Hz and clamped to the max
- [ ] Existing Dart decoder works unchanged (no key changes); optional `attitude` radians key present
- [ ] Builds for a real iOS device with no CoreMotion-related warnings; passes the project's iOS lint/build

## Testing Instructions

1. Confirm the channel-registration pattern and the Dart payload keys before coding (note: match `sensorStream` and the existing `fromMap` exactly).
2. Build and run on a **physical iOS device** (device motion is unavailable on most simulators).

## Assumptions

- Assumed: the channel name is `sensorStream` and the Dart `SensorStreamPayload.fromMap` expects `orientation.alpha/beta/gamma` + `accelerometer.x/y/z` (from the foundation + Flutter-wrapper tasks). Here is the generated coding prompt:

# FEATURE: EventChannel Streaming of Orientation Data at 50–100Hz
# Product: maya-viewer (iOS Native)
# Scope: New Feature
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Implement a Flutter EventChannel that streams device orientation sensor data
from the iOS native layer (Swift) to Dart at a sustained rate of 50–100Hz.

- [ ] Register an EventChannel on the Swift side using a stable channel name
- [ ] Attach a CMMotionManager instance to capture device attitude/rotation data
- [ ] Start DeviceMotion updates at the target interval (0.01s = 100Hz target)
- [ ] Stream orientation data (roll, pitch, yaw) as a Map over the EventChannel sink
- [ ] Stop and clean up the CMMotionManager when the stream is cancelled
- [ ] Expose the channel name as a constant shared with the Dart layer

## Files to Inspect First

1. `ios/Runner/AppDelegate.swift` — understand where plugin/channel registration occurs
2. `ios/Runner/GeneratedPluginRegistrant.m` — confirm whether a plugin pattern or
   inline channel registration is being used
3. `lib/channels/` (or equivalent) — find where existing MethodChannels or
   EventChannels are declared on the Dart side, follow naming conventions
4. `pubspec.yaml` — confirm `sensors_plus` or any competing sensor plugin is NOT
   already registered; if it is, flag before proceeding

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Define the EventChannel name constant

In Swift, declare the channel name at the top of the handler file:

```swift
let kOrientationChannelName = "com.mayasabhaxr.maya_viewer/orientation"
```

Use this exact string on the Dart side too. Do not hardcode it in two separate
places — define it once in Swift and mirror it as a `const String` in Dart.

### Step 2: Create `OrientationStreamHandler.swift`

Create a new file `ios/Runner/OrientationStreamHandler.swift` that implements
`FlutterStreamHandler`. Do not add sensor logic directly into `AppDelegate.swift`.

```swift
import Flutter
import CoreMotion

class OrientationStreamHandler: NSObject, FlutterStreamHandler {
    private let motionManager = CMMotionManager()
    private let updateInterval: TimeInterval = 1.0 / 100.0  // 100Hz

    func onListen(withArguments arguments: Any?,
                  eventSink events: @escaping FlutterEventSink) -> FlutterError? {
        guard motionManager.isDeviceMotionAvailable else {
            events(FlutterError(code: "UNAVAILABLE",
                                message: "DeviceMotion not available on this device",
                                details: nil))
            return nil
        }
        motionManager.deviceMotionUpdateInterval = updateInterval
        motionManager.startDeviceMotionUpdates(to: .main) { [weak self] motion, error in
            guard let motion = motion else { return }
            let attitude = motion.attitude
            events([
                "roll":  attitude.roll,
                "pitch": attitude.pitch,
                "yaw":   attitude.yaw,
                "timestamp": motion.timestamp
            ])
        }
        return nil
    }

    func onCancel(withArguments arguments: Any?) -> FlutterError? {
        motionManager.stopDeviceMotionUpdates()
        return nil
    }
}
```

### Step 3: Register the EventChannel in AppDelegate

In `AppDelegate.swift`, inside `application(_:didFinishLaunchingWithOptions:)`,
after the `GeneratedPluginRegistrant.register` call:

```swift
let controller = window?.rootViewController as! FlutterViewController
let orientationChannel = FlutterEventChannel(
    name: kOrientationChannelName,
    binaryMessenger: controller.binaryMessenger
)
orientationChannel.setStreamHandler(OrientationStreamHandler())
```

Do NOT store `orientationChannel` as a local variable that goes out of scope —
hold a strong reference on the AppDelegate instance if needed.

### Step 4: Dart-side EventChannel subscription

In the appropriate Dart file under `lib/channels/` (create
`orientation_channel.dart` if none exists):

```dart
const _channel = EventChannel('com.mayasabhaxr.maya_viewer/orientation');

Stream<Map<String, double>> get orientationStream =>
    _channel.receiveBroadcastStream().map((event) {
      final data = Map<String, dynamic>.from(event as Map);
      return {
        'roll':      (data['roll']  as num).toDouble(),
        'pitch':     (data['pitch'] as num).toDouble(),
        'yaw':       (data['yaw']   as num).toDouble(),
        'timestamp': (data['timestamp'] as num).toDouble(),
      };
    });
```

Expose this as a singleton or provider — do not instantiate EventChannel
multiple times.

### Step 5: Verify update rate

Log the delta between consecutive timestamps in debug builds to confirm the
rate is sustained between 50–100Hz. Remove the log before merging; use an
`assert` guard or `kDebugMode` flag.

## API / Data Contract

EventChannel name: `com.mayasabhaxr.maya_viewer/orientation`

Payload per event (Map, sent from Swift → Dart):
{

"roll":      Double,   // radians, range [-π, π]

"pitch":     Double,   // radians, range [-π/2, π/2]

"yaw":       Double,   // radians, range [-π, π]

"timestamp": Double    // seconds since device boot (CMDeviceMotion.timestamp)

}

Error codes emitted on the sink:
- `UNAVAILABLE` — CMMotionManager.isDeviceMotionAvailable returned false

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT modify `GeneratedPluginRegistrant.m` — it is auto-generated
- Do NOT touch any existing MethodChannel registrations in AppDelegate
- Do NOT add `sensors_plus` or any third-party sensor Flutter plugin — native
  implementation only
- Do NOT change the camera pipeline code or ARKit session setup if present
- Do NOT alter the Dart `main.dart` or app lifecycle hooks
- Do NOT set `motionManager.showsDeviceMovementDisplay = true` — this is for
  calibration UI, not production

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] `CMMotionManager.isDeviceMotionAvailable` returns false (older iPod Touch,
  simulator) → emit a `FlutterError` with code `UNAVAILABLE` and do not crash
- [ ] Stream is listened to multiple times (hot restart in debug) → call
  `stopDeviceMotionUpdates` in `onCancel` to prevent double-start; guard
  against calling `startDeviceMotionUpdates` if already running
- [ ] App goes to background → `CMMotionManager` continues by default; add
  `UIApplicationDidEnterBackgroundNotification` observer in the handler to
  pause updates and resume on foreground
- [ ] Dart listener cancels mid-stream → `onCancel` must stop the
  motionManager cleanly; verify no EXC_BAD_ACCESS on sink call after cancel
- [ ] iOS Simulator → DeviceMotion is unavailable; emit `UNAVAILABLE` error,
  do not hang

## Constraints

- Use `CMReferenceFrame.xArbitraryCorrectedZVertical` if reference frame is
  needed — do NOT use `.xMagneticNorthZVertical` as it requires compass
  calibration and will show the calibration overlay
- All sink calls must happen on the main queue (`.main` OperationQueue) —
  FlutterEventSink is not thread-safe
- `updateInterval` of `1.0 / 100.0` is the ceiling; actual hardware rate may
  be lower on some devices — do not assert an exact rate, only assert it is
  within 50–100Hz range in tests
- Do not use `startAccelerometerUpdates` or `startGyroUpdates` separately —
  `startDeviceMotionUpdates` fuses both via the sensor fusion algorithm and is
  preferred for attitude data

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] On a real iOS device (iPhone 11 or newer), orientation events are
  received in Dart at 50–100 events per second (verify via timestamp delta log
  in debug mode)
- [ ] Roll, pitch, and yaw values change continuously and correctly as the
  device is physically rotated
- [ ] On iOS Simulator, the stream emits a `FlutterError` with code
  `UNAVAILABLE` and does not crash the app
- [ ] Hot-restarting the Flutter app in debug mode does not cause a double-
  start crash or stale sensor data
- [ ] Cancelling the Dart stream subscription stops CoreMotion updates (verify
  via Instruments: CPU drops, no background sensor activity)
- [ ] App backgrounding pauses sensor updates; foregrounding resumes them
- [ ] No memory leaks from `OrientationStreamHandler` — confirm with Leaks
  instrument after 60s of streaming
- [ ] No Swift compiler warnings, no Dart analysis warnings (`dart analyze`)

## Testing Instructions

1. Run on a physical iPhone — simulator cannot provide DeviceMotion.
2. Subscribe to `orientationStream` in a debug screen and print each event with
   a timestamp; confirm ≥50 events/second over a 5-second window.
3. Rotate the device through all axes and confirm roll/pitch/yaw change in the
   expected direction and range.

   without crash.
6. Run `dart analyze` and `flutter build ios --no-codesign` — both must pass
   clean.

   main thread is not blocked by sensor callbacks.

## Assumptions

  `AppDelegate.swift` is the active delegate (not Objective-C) — if the project

- Assumed: No existing `sensors_plus` or `motion` plugin is in `pubspec.yaml`
  that would conflict — if one exists, remove it or coordinate with the team
  before proceeding.
- Assumed: 100Hz is the target ceiling; 50Hz is the acceptable floor on older
  hardware — if the requirement is a hard 100Hz minimum, this needs hardware

Generate a coding prompt for this task: Low-pass filter equivalent on Swift side — Phase: Native Camera & Sensor Pipeline, Track: iOS Native (Swift)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------->>>>>>>>>>>>



# FEATURE: Low-Pass Filter for Orientation Data on Swift Side
# Product: maya-viewer (iOS Native)
# Scope: New Feature
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Add an exponential moving average (EMA) low-pass filter inside
`OrientationStreamHandler.swift` that smooths roll, pitch, and yaw values
before emitting them over the EventChannel sink. The filter must run entirely
on the Swift side — no filtering logic in Dart.

- [ ] Implement an EMA filter with a configurable alpha coefficient (α)
- [ ] Apply the filter independently to roll, pitch, and yaw each update cycle
- [ ] Default α to `0.15` (aggressive smoothing); make it overridable at
  stream listen time via the `arguments` parameter of `onListen`
- [ ] Handle the angle-wraparound discontinuity for yaw (crosses ±π boundary)
- [ ] Emit filtered values over the existing EventChannel sink — no new channel
- [ ] Add a debug log (gated on `#if DEBUG`) that prints raw vs filtered yaw
  once per second to confirm filter activity

## Files to Inspect First

1. `ios/Runner/OrientationStreamHandler.swift` — this is the only file being
   modified; understand the full `onListen` / `onCancel` lifecycle and where
   CMDeviceMotion callbacks fire before touching anything
2. `lib/channels/orientation_channel.dart` — confirm the Dart subscriber
   consumes roll/pitch/yaw as `double`; the payload shape must not change
3. `ios/Runner/AppDelegate.swift` — confirm how `OrientationStreamHandler` is
   instantiated; check whether `arguments` from `onListen` is already forwarded
   or needs to be plumbed through

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Add EMA filter state to OrientationStreamHandler

Declare three optional `Double` properties to store the previous filtered
values. Use optionals so the first sample initialises the filter without
producing a biased output:

```swift
private var filteredRoll:  Double? = nil
private var filteredPitch: Double? = nil
private var filteredYaw:   Double? = nil
private var alpha: Double = 0.15
```

Place these directly below the existing `motionManager` property declaration.

### Step 2: Parse alpha from onListen arguments

At the top of `onListen(withArguments:eventSink:)`, before starting
DeviceMotion updates, read the optional alpha override:

```swift
if let args = arguments as? [String: Any],
   let customAlpha = args["alpha"] as? Double,
   customAlpha > 0.0, customAlpha <= 1.0 {
    alpha = customAlpha
}
```

Clamp silently — do not emit an error for an out-of-range alpha; fall back to
the default `0.15` instead.

### Step 3: Implement the EMA helper — handle yaw wraparound

Add a private method to the class:

```swift
private func emaFilter(previous: Double?, raw: Double,
                        alpha: Double, isAngle: Bool = false) -> Double {
    guard let prev = previous else { return raw }
    if isAngle {
        // Shortest-path interpolation to avoid ±π discontinuity
        var delta = raw - prev
        if delta >  Double.pi { delta -= 2 * Double.pi }
        if delta < -Double.pi { delta += 2 * Double.pi }
        return prev + alpha * delta
    }
    return alpha * raw + (1.0 - alpha) * prev
}
```

`isAngle: true` must be passed for yaw only. Roll and pitch are bounded
[-π/2, π/2] and do not wrap, so use the standard EMA path for them.

### Step 4: Apply the filter inside the DeviceMotion callback

Replace the raw attitude assignments in the `startDeviceMotionUpdates` closure
with filtered equivalents:

```swift
let raw = motion.attitude

filteredRoll  = emaFilter(previous: filteredRoll,  raw: raw.roll,
                           alpha: alpha)
filteredPitch = emaFilter(previous: filteredPitch, raw: raw.pitch,
                           alpha: alpha)
filteredYaw   = emaFilter(previous: filteredYaw,   raw: raw.yaw,
                           alpha: alpha, isAngle: true)

events([
    "roll":      filteredRoll!,
    "pitch":     filteredPitch!,
    "yaw":       filteredYaw!,
    "timestamp": motion.timestamp
])
```

Force-unwrap is safe here because `emaFilter` always returns a non-optional.

### Step 5: Reset filter state in onCancel

Add reset lines inside `onCancel` so a re-subscription starts clean:

```swift
filteredRoll  = nil
filteredPitch = nil
filteredYaw   = nil
alpha = 0.15
```

### Step 6: Add gated debug logging

Below the `events(...)` call, add a once-per-second log using a frame counter:

```swift
#if DEBUG
privateFrameCount += 1
if privateFrameCount % 100 == 0 {   // ~1s at 100Hz
    print("[OrientationFilter] raw yaw: \(raw.yaw), filtered: \(filteredYaw!)")
}
#endif
```

Declare `private var privateFrameCount: Int = 0` with the other state
properties and reset it to `0` in `onCancel`.

## API / Data Contract

EventChannel payload — shape is UNCHANGED from the previous task:
{

"roll":      Double,   // filtered, radians

"pitch":     Double,   // filtered, radians

"yaw":       Double,   // filtered, radians — wraparound-safe

"timestamp": Double    // raw CMDeviceMotion.timestamp, unfiltered

}

`onListen` arguments (optional Map from Dart caller):
{

"alpha": Double   // 0.0 < alpha ≤ 1.0; omit to use default 0.15

}

Dart callers passing a custom alpha:
```dart
_channel.receiveBroadcastStream({'alpha': 0.25})
```

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT add a new EventChannel or MethodChannel — filtering goes inside the
  existing `OrientationStreamHandler`, not a wrapper
- Do NOT modify `AppDelegate.swift` unless the `arguments` plumbing requires
  it — and even then, only touch the `OrientationStreamHandler` initialisation
  line
- Do NOT change the payload key names (`roll`, `pitch`, `yaw`, `timestamp`) —
  Dart consumers depend on these exact strings
- Do NOT apply the EMA to `timestamp` — pass the raw hardware timestamp always
- Do NOT touch the Dart-side `orientation_channel.dart` or any other Dart file
- Do NOT modify `CMMotionManager` update interval or reference frame settings
  established in the previous task

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] First sample after `onListen` → `filteredRoll/Pitch/Yaw` are `nil`; EMA
  returns raw value directly (no bias from a zero initial state)
- [ ] Yaw crossing the ±π boundary (e.g. 3.10 → -3.10) → shortest-path delta
  interpolation in `emaFilter(isAngle: true)` prevents a violent spike
- [ ] Dart passes `alpha: 0.0` or `alpha: 1.5` → clamp silently to default
  `0.15`; do not emit a `FlutterError`
- [ ] Stream is cancelled and re-subscribed within the same app session →
  `onCancel` resets all state to `nil`; new subscription starts fresh
- [ ] Device held perfectly still → filtered values must converge to and stay
  at the raw values within ~20 frames; verify no creep or drift
- [ ] Very fast rotation (>180°/s) → filter should not lose tracking; verify
  yaw wraparound path handles multi-frame large deltas without oscillation

## Constraints

- `emaFilter` must be a pure function with no side effects — it reads only its
  arguments and returns a `Double`; all state mutation happens at the call site
- Alpha `0.15` means the new sample contributes 15% weight; higher alpha =
  less smoothing. This is the correct EMA convention — do not invert it
- The debug `#if DEBUG` block must not exist in release builds; do not use a
  runtime flag as a substitute for the compile-time guard
- Do not use `simd` or `Accelerate` framework for this filter — the EMA
  implementation is arithmetic only and must remain readable for future tuning

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] With `alpha: 0.15` (default), rapidly shaking the device produces
  visually smooth roll/pitch/yaw values in the Dart debug screen — no jitter
  visible at human timescales
- [ ] With `alpha: 1.0` passed via `onListen` arguments, filtered values equal
  raw values exactly (no smoothing — confirms the formula is correct)
- [ ] Rotating the device past the yaw ±π boundary does not produce a spike or
  jump in the filtered yaw value — transition is continuous
- [ ] Cancelling the stream and re-subscribing produces identical behaviour to
  a fresh app launch — no stale state carried over
- [ ] The `timestamp` field in the emitted payload is the raw
  `CMDeviceMotion.timestamp`, not filtered (verify: values increment by ~0.01s)
- [ ] `#if DEBUG` log prints to Xcode console in debug builds and is absent in
  a release archive (`flutter build ios --release` — no print calls in binary)
- [ ] No Swift compiler warnings introduced
- [ ] `dart analyze` passes clean — no changes to Dart files required; confirm
  by running it and checking zero new issues

## Testing Instructions

1. Build and run on a physical iPhone in debug mode (Simulator has no motion).
2. Subscribe to `orientationStream` without arguments — confirm default alpha
   `0.15` smoothing is visible: shake the phone rapidly and observe that
   roll/pitch/yaw values in the debug overlay change smoothly, not erratically.
3. Subscribe with `{'alpha': 1.0}` — confirm filtered values match raw values
   printed in the debug log (raw yaw == filtered yaw within floating-point
   precision).
4. Slowly rotate the device in yaw through a full 360° — watch for any
   discontinuity at the ±π crossing; there must be none.

   first few frames are not biased toward the last known filtered value.

   print statements appear (grep for `OrientationFilter` in the build log).
7. Run `dart analyze` — confirm zero new warnings or errors.

## Assumptions

- Assumed: `OrientationStreamHandler.swift` already exists and is wired up from
  the previous EventChannel task — if it does not exist yet, complete that task
  first; this prompt is a direct continuation.
- Assumed: The Dart `receiveBroadcastStream()` call can be updated to pass an

[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[[ 4 ]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]]


Generate a coding prompt for this task: Blur detection (CIFilter or Metal — same Laplacian approach as Android) — Phase: Native Camera & Sensor Pipeline, Track: iOS Native (Swift)

------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------->>>>>>>



# FEATURE: Blur Detection via Laplacian Variance (CIFilter — iOS Native Swift)
# Product: maya-viewer (iOS Native)
# Scope: New Feature
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Implement a blur detection pipeline on the iOS native Swift side that computes
the Laplacian variance of a camera frame and classifies it as blurry or sharp.
This must be functionally equivalent to the Android Laplacian approach already
in the codebase. The result is exposed to Flutter via a MethodChannel call
(not a stream) — the Dart layer calls `detectBlur` with a frame, receives a
score and a boolean classification.

- [ ] Create `BlurDetector.swift` with a static `detect(pixelBuffer:)` method
  that returns a `(score: Double, isBlurry: Bool)` tuple
- [ ] Implement the Laplacian convolution using `CIFilter` with kernel
  `CIConvolution3X3`; fall back to a CPU-based Accelerate vDSP path only if
  `CIConvolution3X3` is unavailable (it is available on all iOS 9+ devices —
  this is a compile-time guard, not a runtime check)
- [ ] Downsample the input `CVPixelBuffer` to 320×240 grayscale before
  applying the convolution to bound CPU/GPU cost per frame
- [ ] Compute variance of the Laplacian output image as the blur score
- [ ] Apply a default threshold of `80.0` — scores below this are classified
  `isBlurry: true`; make the threshold overridable via a single constant
  `BlurDetector.threshold` that callers can mutate
- [ ] Register a `MethodChannel` handler for the channel name
  `com.mayasabhaxr.maya_viewer/blur` with method `detectBlur`; it accepts a
  raw BGRA `FlutterStandardTypedData` byte buffer + width + height from Dart
  and returns `{"score": Double, "isBlurry": Bool}`
- [ ] Gate all `print` / debug logging behind `#if DEBUG`

## Files to Inspect First

1. `ios/Runner/AppDelegate.swift` — understand the MethodChannel registration
   pattern already used (e.g. for the orientation channel); mirror exactly
2. `ios/Runner/OrientationStreamHandler.swift` — review Swift file conventions
   (naming, access control, file structure) used in this codebase before
   creating a new Swift file
3. `lib/channels/` — locate the Dart file where existing channel names are
   declared; add `blur_channel.dart` following the same pattern
4. `android/app/src/main/kotlin/.../BlurDetector.kt` (or equivalent) — read
   the Android Laplacian implementation to confirm the kernel weights and
   variance formula so the iOS output is numerically comparable

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Create BlurDetector.swift

Create `ios/Runner/BlurDetector.swift`. This file owns all image processing
logic and has zero Flutter imports — it is pure AVFoundation/CoreImage/
Accelerate. Do not put MethodChannel wiring inside this file.

```swift
import CoreImage
import Accelerate

struct BlurDetector {

    // Callers may override before first use; not thread-safe — set once at startup
    static var threshold: Double = 80.0

    /// Laplacian kernel matching Android implementation:
    /// [ 0,  1,  0]
    /// [ 1, -4,  1]
    /// [ 0,  1,  0]
    private static let laplacianWeights: [CGFloat] = [
         0,  1,  0,
         1, -4,  1,
         0,  1,  0
    ]

    static func detect(pixelBuffer: CVPixelBuffer) -> (score: Double, isBlurry: Bool) {
        guard let gray = toGrayscaleCIImage(pixelBuffer: pixelBuffer),
              let laplacian = applyLaplacian(to: gray),
              let score = computeVariance(of: laplacian) else {
            // Fail open: treat undecidable frames as sharp so callers aren't
            // blocked on pipeline errors
            return (score: Double.nan, isBlurry: false)
        }
        return (score: score, isBlurry: score < threshold)
    }
}
```

### Step 2: Implement toGrayscaleCIImage

Inside `BlurDetector.swift`, add the downsample + grayscale helper. Downsample
to 320×240 before colour conversion to keep the convolution cost constant
regardless of camera resolution:

```swift
private static let ciContext = CIContext(options: [.useSoftwareRenderer: false])
private static let targetSize = CGSize(width: 320, height: 240)

private static func toGrayscaleCIImage(pixelBuffer: CVPixelBuffer) -> CIImage? {
    let original = CIImage(cvPixelBuffer: pixelBuffer)
    let scaleX = targetSize.width  / original.extent.width
    let scaleY = targetSize.height / original.extent.height
    let scaled = original.transformed(by: CGAffineTransform(scaleX: scaleX, y: scaleY))
    // CIPhotoEffectNoir converts to luminance without requiring a manual matrix
    return scaled.applyingFilter("CIPhotoEffectNoir")
}
```

### Step 3: Implement applyLaplacian using CIConvolution3X3

```swift
private static func applyLaplacian(to image: CIImage) -> CIImage? {
    let weights = CIVector(values: laplacianWeights, count: 9)
    return image.applyingFilter("CIConvolution3X3", parameters: [
        "inputWeights": weights,
        "inputBias": 0.0
    ])
}
```

`CIConvolution3X3` normalises pixel values to [0,1] float internally. The
output pixel values represent the magnitude of second-order spatial derivative.

### Step 4: Implement computeVariance

Render the Laplacian CIImage to a pixel buffer, then use vDSP to compute
variance efficiently. Do NOT use a nested pixel loop — vDSP is mandatory here
for performance:

```swift
private static func computeVariance(of image: CIImage) -> Double? {
    let width  = Int(image.extent.width)
    let height = Int(image.extent.height)
    let count  = width * height

    var bitmap = [UInt8](repeating: 0, count: count)
    ciContext.render(image,
                     toBitmap: &bitmap,
                     rowBytes: width,
                     bounds: image.extent,
                     format: .R8,
                     colorSpace: CGColorSpaceCreateDeviceGray())

    // Convert UInt8 → Float for vDSP
    var floats = [Float](repeating: 0, count: count)
    vDSP_vfltu8(&bitmap, 1, &floats, 1, vDSP_Length(count))

    var mean: Float = 0
    vDSP_meanv(floats, 1, &mean, vDSP_Length(count))

    // Subtract mean
    var negMean = -mean
    vDSP_vsadd(floats, 1, &negMean, &floats, 1, vDSP_Length(count))

    // Square
    vDSP_vsq(floats, 1, &floats, 1, vDSP_Length(count))

    // Mean of squares = variance
    var variance: Float = 0
    vDSP_meanv(floats, 1, &variance, vDSP_Length(count))

    return Double(variance)
}
```

### Step 5: Create BlurMethodChannelHandler.swift

Create `ios/Runner/BlurMethodChannelHandler.swift`. This file owns only the
Flutter ↔ Swift bridge. It converts the incoming Dart byte buffer into a
`CVPixelBuffer`, calls `BlurDetector.detect`, and returns the result dict.

```swift
import Flutter
import CoreVideo

final class BlurMethodChannelHandler {

    static let channelName = "com.mayasabhaxr.maya_viewer/blur"

    func handle(call: FlutterMethodCall, result: @escaping FlutterResult) {
        guard call.method == "detectBlur" else {
            result(FlutterMethodNotImplemented)
            return
        }
        guard let args   = call.arguments as? [String: Any],
              let bytes  = (args["bytes"] as? FlutterStandardTypedData)?.data,
              let width  = args["width"]  as? Int,
              let height = args["height"] as? Int else {
            result(FlutterError(code: "INVALID_ARGS",
                                message: "detectBlur requires bytes, width, height",
                                details: nil))
            return
        }
        guard let pixelBuffer = makePixelBuffer(bytes: bytes,
                                                width: width,
                                                height: height) else {
            result(FlutterError(code: "BUFFER_ERROR",
                                message: "Failed to create CVPixelBuffer from bytes",
                                details: nil))
            return
        }
        let detection = BlurDetector.detect(pixelBuffer: pixelBuffer)
        result(["score": detection.score, "isBlurry": detection.isBlurry])
    }

    // BGRA byte array → CVPixelBuffer (no copy — caller must keep `bytes` alive
    // for the duration of the call, which is synchronous here so this is safe)
    private func makePixelBuffer(bytes: Data, width: Int, height: Int) -> CVPixelBuffer? {
        var pb: CVPixelBuffer?
        bytes.withUnsafeBytes { ptr in
            guard let base = ptr.baseAddress else { return }
            CVPixelBufferCreateWithBytes(
                nil, width, height,
                kCVPixelFormatType_32BGRA,
                UnsafeMutableRawPointer(mutating: base),
                width * 4,   // bytesPerRow for BGRA
                nil, nil, nil,
                &pb
            )
        }
        return pb
    }
}
```

### Step 6: Register the MethodChannel in AppDelegate

In `AppDelegate.swift`, inside `application(_:didFinishLaunchingWithOptions:)`,
after existing channel registrations:

```swift
let blurHandler = BlurMethodChannelHandler()
let blurChannel = FlutterMethodChannel(
    name: BlurMethodChannelHandler.channelName,
    binaryMessenger: controller.binaryMessenger
)
blurChannel.setMethodCallHandler(blurHandler.handle)
```

Hold `blurHandler` and `blurChannel` as strong properties on the AppDelegate
to prevent ARC deallocation.

### Step 7: Dart-side channel wrapper

Create `lib/channels/blur_channel.dart`:

```dart
import 'dart:typed_data';
import 'package:flutter/services.dart';

const _channel = MethodChannel('com.mayasabhaxr.maya_viewer/blur');

/// [bytes] must be raw BGRA, [width] × [height] pixels.
Future<({double score, bool isBlurry})> detectBlur({
  required Uint8List bytes,
  required int width,
  required int height,
}) async {
  final result = await _channel.invokeMapMethod<String, dynamic>(
    'detectBlur',
    {'bytes': bytes, 'width': width, 'height': height},
  );
  return (
    score:    (result!['score']    as num).toDouble(),
    isBlurry: result['isBlurry']  as bool,
  );
}
```

## API / Data Contract

MethodChannel name: `com.mayasabhaxr.maya_viewer/blur`
Method: `detectBlur`

Dart → Swift arguments (Map):
{

"bytes":  FlutterStandardTypedData   // raw BGRA pixel data, width × height × 4 bytes

"width":  Int                        // pixel width of the frame

"height": Int                        // pixel height of the frame

}

Swift → Dart return (Map):
{

"score":    Double   // Laplacian variance; higher = sharper

"isBlurry": Bool     // true when score < BlurDetector.threshold (default 80.0)

}

Error codes returned via `FlutterError`:
- `INVALID_ARGS` — missing or wrong-typed arguments
- `BUFFER_ERROR` — `CVPixelBuffer` creation failed from the provided bytes

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT modify `OrientationStreamHandler.swift` or the orientation EventChannel
- Do NOT modify `AppDelegate.swift` beyond adding the two blur channel lines in
  Step 6; do not touch existing channel registrations
- Do NOT add Vision framework or CoreML — this task uses CIFilter only
- Do NOT implement streaming blur scores — only the single-call MethodChannel
  pattern is in scope; a continuous stream is a separate future task
- Do NOT change the Dart `orientation_channel.dart` or any other existing Dart
  channel file
- Do NOT process frames at full camera resolution inside `BlurDetector` — the
  320×240 downsample in Step 2 is mandatory; removing it will cause jank

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] `CVPixelBuffer` creation fails (malformed byte length, zero dimensions) →
  return `FlutterError(code: "BUFFER_ERROR")`; do not crash
- [ ] `CIContext.render` produces all-zero pixels (e.g. black frame from camera
  warm-up) → variance will be `0.0`, which is below threshold; this is correct
  behaviour — black/empty frames are classified blurry; do not special-case it
- [ ] `BlurDetector.detect` is called concurrently from multiple Dart isolates →
  `ciContext` is a `static let` and `CIContext` is thread-safe; `threshold` is
  a `static var` and is not thread-safe — document this in a comment; do not
  add a lock (not in scope)
- [ ] Frame byte count does not equal `width × height × 4` → `CVPixelBufferCreateWithBytes`
  will produce undefined behaviour; validate byte count before calling and
  return `INVALID_ARGS` if mismatched
- [ ] iOS Simulator (no physical camera) → the channel handler and
  `BlurDetector` must still compile and respond without crashing when given
  synthetic test bytes; confirmed by the unit test in Testing Instructions

## Constraints

- The Laplacian kernel weights must be exactly `[0, 1, 0, 1, -4, 1, 0, 1, 0]`
  (matching Android) — do not use a different kernel (e.g. the 8-neighbour
  variant `[-1,-1,-1,-1,8,-1,-1,-1,-1]`) without explicit sign-off
- `CIContext` must be created once as a `static let` — never inside the
  per-frame `detect` call; creating a new `CIContext` per frame causes severe
  memory pressure
- Variance must be computed with vDSP (`vDSP_meanv` + `vDSP_vsq`) — a Swift
  `map/reduce` loop over 76,800 pixels (320×240) is too slow for real-time use
- `BlurDetector` must have no Flutter imports — it must be independently unit-
  testable without a Flutter engine
- The default threshold `80.0` is intentionally tuned for 8-bit Laplacian
  variance on 320×240 grayscale. Do not normalise the variance to [0,1] before
  comparing against threshold — the Android side does not normalise either

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] Pointing the camera at a flat in-focus surface returns `isBlurry: false`
  with `score > 80.0` on an iPhone 11 or newer in good lighting
- [ ] Covering the lens with a finger or severely defocusing the camera returns
  `isBlurry: true` with `score < 80.0`
- [ ] A synthetically sharp test image (high-contrast checkerboard) and a
  synthetically blurry one (Gaussian-blurred version of the same) produce scores
  that differ by at least 2× when passed as raw bytes via the MethodChannel
  on Simulator
- [ ] `BlurDetector.threshold = 200.0` set before calling `detectBlur` causes
  previously sharp frames to be classified `isBlurry: true` — confirms
  threshold override works
- [ ] Passing a byte array where `bytes.count ≠ width × height × 4` returns
  `FlutterError(code: "INVALID_ARGS")` without crashing
- [ ] Calling `detectBlur` 30 times in rapid succession (tight Dart loop) does
  not increase memory usage by more than 5MB (verified in Xcode Memory Report)
  — confirms `CIContext` is reused, not re-created
- [ ] `flutter build ios --no-codesign` (release mode) compiles without warnings
- [ ] `dart analyze` reports zero new issues

## Testing Instructions

1. Physical device test — sharp vs blurry:
   Run the app on an iPhone, call `detectBlur` from a test screen with a live
   camera frame. Point at a sharp text document → log score. Cover lens → log
   score. Confirm the scores differ by >2× and the `isBlurry` flag flips.

2. Simulator unit test — synthetic bytes:
   In a debug test button, construct a 320×240 BGRA `Uint8List` filled with
   alternating 0/255 values (simulates a sharp checkerboard). Call `detectBlur`.
   Confirm `score > 80.0`. Then fill with a constant value (flat grey = no
   edges) and call again. Confirm `score < 5.0`.

3. Threshold override test:
   Set `BlurDetector.threshold = 100000.0` via a debug button before calling
   `detectBlur` on a sharp frame. Confirm `isBlurry: true` is returned.
   Reset to `80.0` and confirm `isBlurry: false` is returned for the same frame.

4. Invalid args test:
   Call the MethodChannel directly with a byte array of length `10` but
   `width: 320, height: 240`. Confirm `FlutterError(code: "INVALID_ARGS")` is
   received in Dart without a native crash.

5. Memory test:
   In Xcode Instruments (Allocations), call `detectBlur` in a tight loop 100×
   with a valid 320×240 buffer. Confirm no monotonic memory growth after the
   first 5 warm-up calls.

6. Build test:
   Run `flutter build ios --no-codesign` and `dart analyze`. Both must pass
   clean with zero new warnings or errors.

## Assumptions

- Assumed: The Android Laplacian implementation uses the 4-neighbour kernel
  `[0,1,0,1,-4,1,0,1,0]` and computes variance of the raw (unnormalised) 8-bit
  output. If the Android kernel or variance formula differs, update
  `laplacianWeights` and the variance computation to match before finalising
  the iOS threshold of `80.0`.
- Assumed: Dart callers pass BGRA byte order (matching `kCVPixelFormatType_32BGRA`).
  If the camera pipeline produces RGBA or YUV bytes, `makePixelBuffer` needs a
  different `CVPixelFormatType` constant and this prompt needs updating.
- Assumed: Blur detection is called on-demand (per captured frame) from Dart,
  not continuously from a background thread. If it needs to run on every camera
  frame natively without a Dart call, the architecture shifts to an
  `AVCaptureVideoDataOutputSampleBufferDelegate` and this becomes a streaming
  task — out of scope here.

# FEATURE: Photo Capture to HEIC/JPEG via AVCapturePhotoOutput
# Product: maya-viewer (iOS Native)
# Scope: New Feature
# Priority: Critical

---

## Task Description

Implement a photo capture pipeline on the iOS Swift side using
`AVCapturePhotoOutput` that captures a still image in HEIC (preferred) or
JPEG (fallback), writes it to a temp file, and returns the absolute file path
to Flutter via a MethodChannel. The capture session must be set up, started,
and torn down entirely in Swift — Dart only calls `capturePhoto` and receives
a file path string back.

- [ ] Create `CameraCapturePipeline.swift` that owns the `AVCaptureSession`,
  `AVCaptureDeviceInput` (rear camera), and `AVCapturePhotoOutput`
- [ ] Implement session lifecycle: `start()` and `stop()` methods that run the
  session on a dedicated background serial queue
- [ ] Implement `capturePhoto(format:completion:)` — `format` is `"heic"` or
  `"jpeg"`; use HEIC when `AVCapturePhotoOutput.availablePhotoCodecTypes`
  contains `.heic`, otherwise silently fall back to JPEG regardless of the
  requested format
- [ ] Write the captured photo data to `NSTemporaryDirectory()` with a unique
  UUID filename and the appropriate extension (`.heic` or `.jpg`)
- [ ] Expose the capture via MethodChannel `com.mayasabhaxr.maya_viewer/camera`
  with method `capturePhoto`; accept optional argument `{"format": "heic"|"jpeg"}`;
  return the absolute temp file path as a `String` on success or a
  `FlutterError` on failure
- [ ] Register the MethodChannel in `AppDelegate.swift` following the same
  pattern used for the blur and orientation channels
- [ ] Create `lib/channels/camera_channel.dart` on the Dart side

## Files to Inspect First

   EventChannel handlers are registered; mirror the exact pattern (strong-
   reference storage, registration order, `binaryMessenger` usage)
2. `ios/Runner/BlurMethodChannelHandler.swift` — use as the structural template
